They were back by the river, because water understands superpositions better than conference tables. Joshua skipped a stone; it hopped twice and failed, which is still a career. Rings spread, argued with each other, and settled into the larger argument of the current.

“Has. Is. Maybe,” Michael said. “People want one verb for God and a fistfight for the runners-up. Has a creation, is the field, maybe approaches in experience without a warrant. If I crown only is, I get accused of erasing personhood and prayer. If I crown only has, I smuggle an outside owner again. If I crown only maybe, I get a shrug that never risks love.”

Joshua dusted his hands. “So both-and with three hands.”

“Three hands,” Michael agreed. “The monogram didn’t force a single altar. The axes didn’t force a single direction. The fuck-life couplet didn’t force active or passive. This is the same muscle. Uncollapsed wave. You both did and didn’t, in different offices of language.”

A rower slid past, neat as a good distinction. Oars kissed water and left it for the next stroke. Nothing insisted the river choose a single story about what wetness meant.

Joshua: “Bookstore plain.”

Michael tried. “God has—if has-language helps you love and thank and not treat people like spare parts of a machine. God is—if is-language keeps you from inventing a landlord beyond being. God maybe—if maybe-language keeps you humble when experience goes quiet. The wave doesn’t have to collapse for you to make tea.”

“Phew,” Joshua said, and they both laughed, the laugh of men putting down a stone without pretending the hill is gone.

Michael sent Cameron the three verbs like a peace offering disguised as a report:

Has / is / maybe. Uncollapsed. Three offices. No fistfight required.

Cameron: This is the most reasonable you’ve been while still being insufferable. I can live here.

Michael: High praise.

Cameron: It’s the only praise. Don’t spend it all at once.

They walked under bridges where graffiti offered simpler theologies in spray that would outlast several governments and still lose to damp. Michael felt fond of simplicity without surrendering complexity—another uncollapsed pair.

“I used to think maybe was cowardice,” he said. “Now it feels like manners toward mystery. Not the maybe of never landing. The maybe of not kidnapping God into one grammar.”

“Land where you must,” Joshua said. “Ethics. Care. The no-sin refusal of exile. The dual altar honesty. Those can land. The metaphysical pronoun can stay a chord instead of a single note.”

Michael watched light on water—particle if you liked particle stories, wave if you liked wave stories, wet if you fell in. A swan ignored them with aristocratic skill. “I both did and didn’t prove it,” he said. “Frame-dependent. Novel as long recognition. Cameron’s court still open for business.”

“And your court?” Joshua asked.

“Open for living,” Michael said. “Which is a different docket.”

Evening at the desk, he wrote the three words without circling a winner. HAS. IS. MAYBE. Under them, not as a slogan for strangers but as a note to his own future panic: uncollapsed on purpose.

He thought of readers who would need one door more than another. He hoped the book could be a building with several doors, light on when open, no ticket for worthiness—like a certain hall with floor polish and broken crayon animals. Some would enter through thank-you. Some through the closed map. Some through the honest quiet when experience goes flat and false trumpets would be a kind of violence.

Joshua texted: Tomorrow you wake the rest of us. Gently.

Michael: Gently. Also firmly. Both.

Joshua: Always both.

Michael turned off the lamp. In the dark the three verbs glowed like afterimages—not competing neon, shared phosphorescence. He practised saying them without ranking, the way a musician hums a chord instead of three rival solos. Has—for the thank-you that needs an addressee. Is—for the closed map that will not reinstall a beyond. Maybe—for the nights when honesty refuses to fake a trumpet.

Cameron again, late: Biscuits still on for the wake-up chapter?

Michael: Biscuits and an empty chair and no forced collapse.

Cameron: I can work with that.

God has.

God is.

God maybe.

Phew.

The stone of forced choice stayed on the riverbed where Joshua’s skip had ended, and the river—having no outside to escape to—continued anyway, which was its own kind of amen: not a settled verb, a moving one, carrying light and rubbish and the occasional swan toward a sea that was still, somehow, only more of the same field wearing a saltier coat.

Chapter 64

The Rest Of Us Wake Up

925 words

Morning did its usual miracle of pretending to be new.

Michael woke and, for once, did not rush the experiment of trying not to be here. He let here be here. Cold floor. Soft electric hum. The monogram on the wall. The notebook fat with a week that had refused burial. Tea first, for one body, then the phone—not as a pulpit, as a kitchen table that happened to fit in a pocket.

I think the next bit isn’t about me noticing, he sent. It’s about the rest of us. Reader included. Wake gently.

Joshua: Yes. Invite, don’t conscript. Pub table later. Ordinary light.

Cameron: If you’re about to found a morning cult, I want biscuits and a right of refusal.

Michael: Biscuits granted. Refusal sacred.

Cameron: Then proceed.

He walked the long route through town with no headphones. Faces. Dogs. A delivery driver who did not know he had once been a laboratory for Michael’s dying exile-fantasy. Shop shutters going up like eyelids. The rest of us, waking in the ordinary sense—and maybe, under that, in the sense the book needed.

Not: you idiots finally see.
Yes: you were always in the room; the room is what is; the jokes were load-bearing; the poles are both; the name of the whole can look like how life continues; there is no final elsewhere to dump the unbearable—including you when you fail.

He stopped outside the community hall. Light off. Still a doorway. He did not need it open to know the category held. A pigeon inspected the threshold with professional interest and left without converting.

Joshua met him at the scarred pub table that had survived more revelations than polish. Same corner. Same brewery logo on the napkins—cousins of the one still folded in Michael’s pocket like a treaty. Michael almost laughed at the continuity; then he did laugh, and Joshua looked pleased.

“For the ones still asleep,” Joshua said. “Not insult—description. I slept through decades of my own life in good company.”

Michael turned a mug. Steam rose like a small weather system. “I don’t get to crow. Verse forty-two is still in my eye. I only get to set the table.”

“Then set it.”

Michael spoke plain, the hardest dialect, practising for a final chapter that would need the same muscles tomorrow:

“Your experience of this moment exists. That’s the only thing that doesn’t need a committee. From there the chain we’ve walked—nothing as true outside, no pure cut, hell-as-exit failing, sin-as-standing-outside failing, generative poles both, total without a locker room, monogram with two altars in one ink—isn’t a private fever. It’s available. Recognition, not recruitment. You can refuse. Refusal happens here too.”

Joshua’s eyes shone with that brother-look: pride mixed with finally.

“And the dirty bit,” Joshua said, fondly, “because God is not a coward.”

Michael almost bottled it. Bookstore shelves are full of books that almost bottled it.

He didn’t—not entirely. He tapped the napkin in his pocket through the cloth. “Tomorrow we say it with the drawing on the table and Cameron’s empty chair for the court that keeps us honest. Tonight I only wake the corridor. The rest of us—including me every morning—get to notice what cannot be un-noticed once seen.”

A woman at the next table glanced over, decided this was either a book club or a soft coup, and returned to her scone. Michael silently dedicated the chapter to her—the patron saint of people who stay for the jam and leave the metaphysics on the side plate.

His phone stirred.

Cameron: I felt a disturbance in the sarcasm. You nearly preached.

Michael: Nearly. Invitation only. Biscuits tomorrow if you come.

Cameron: I’ll come. Empty chair optional. Someone has to say motte-and-bailey at the end so the novel doesn’t get drunk on itself.

Michael: You’re part of the heartbeat, you know.

Cameron: Disgusting. True. See you there.

They finished their drinks. Outside, morning had become more morning—that second wave when the post arrives and the light stops apologising. Joshua walked him as far as the corner where the pavement chooses two ways and pretends the choice matters more than it does.

“So now what?” Joshua asked.

Michael grinned, which is a kind of amen in draft form.

“Now we live the day,” he said. “And then we sit down and say the whole thing plain—napkin, brothers, empty chair, cold floor on day one—and we let the last line do the carrying.”

“You already know the last line,” Joshua said.

“I’ve always known it,” Michael said. “I just had to become someone who could mean it.”

He went home, washed a plate, answered an email, stood in the doorway of his own life without demanding a hatch. The monogram did not glow. It did not need to. It had entered the quieter office of true things: not spectacle, furniture—something you walk past and still live under.

The rest of us were waking in a thousand kitchens.

Including, if you are reading this, the particular skull you cannot leave—

which was never exile.

Which was always the only room.

Which was, and is, about to be told a joke serious enough to build a world on.

He set two mugs out for tomorrow without knowing who would use the second—Joshua, Cameron, or only the idea of company. Hospitality toward the waking. Not a cult. A table with steam rising and an empty chair that was not emptiness, only space left on purpose.

Then he slept inside the only room, which had always been large enough for the rest of us.

Chapter 65

A Funny Old Life

913 words

By the end, the arguments had all been to the same pub.

Not the same opinion. Pubs are full of opinions, most of them sticky. The same room.

Michael sat with Joshua at a scarred table that had survived more revelations than polish. Cameron’s empty chair still held the ghost of forty-two—an ordinary number, proud of its ordinariness, like a man who refuses a cape on principle.

Outside, morning did its usual miracle of pretending to be new.

Joshua turned his mug. ‘Say it plain. For the ones still asleep.’

Michael had learned that plain was the hardest dialect.

‘Your experience of this moment exists,’ he said. ‘That’s the only thing that doesn’t need a committee. From there: nothing can’t be a real outside, because even naming it is something. So existence isn’t a team with an away kit. It’s one field. No true cut. No pure elsewhere. Which means hell-as-exit is a booking error. Which means sin-as-standing-outside-the-whole is the same booking error in a moral hat.’

Joshua’s eyes shone with that brother-look: pride mixed with finally.

‘And the dirty bit,’ Joshua said, fondly. ‘Because God is not a coward.’

Michael almost bottled it. Bookstore shelves are full of books that almost bottled it.

He didn’t.

‘If the whole can’t be split,’ he said, ‘then the generative poles aren’t a war with a winner. Entering and receiving. Making and holding. The lot. God has no gender because the total doesn’t need a locker room. The old monogram—Alpha and Omega, beginning and end—isn’t decoration. It’s the claim of span. And in the fonts we actually live in, those letters…’

He sketched on a napkin, ridiculous and precise:

α==ω

Joshua snorted tea. ‘There it is.’

‘Cock and balls if you read it one way,’ Michael said, cheeks hot, voice steady. ‘Boobs and the whole soft geometry of the other altar if you read it the other. Same ink. Same claim. Not either/or. The light chooses; the field doesn’t. Length, girth, depth, breadth—every axis—because existence doesn’t have a preferred direction for the joke.’

A woman at the next table glanced over, decided this was either theology or a graphic design emergency, and returned to her scone.

Cameron, in memory, muttered motte-and-bailey. Michael smiled at the empty chair.

‘You’re right in classical court,’ he told the ghost. ‘It’s polysemy. It’s juxtaposition. It’s a definition game if you only count one temple at a time. And also—because we’re not only in classical court—it’s what happens when the map of the whole is forced to wear a body. The strong claim is a bailey. The funny claim survives cross-examination. The novel was the long way of making the funny claim undeniable as recognition.’

Joshua raised his mug. ‘No sin.’

‘No sin as outside,’ Michael said. ‘No stain that isn’t already the play of the only thing that is. That isn’t permission to be cruel. It’s the end of the fantasy that cruelty happens elsewhere than God. Including us. Especially us.’

They sat with that. It was heavy. It was light. Both.

‘We can enjoy being fucked by life,’ Joshua said softly, ‘and we can fuck life.’

‘Both,’ Michael said.

‘I don’t have a big dick and I am God,’ Joshua added, deadpan. ‘But it’s both-and.’

‘The wave doesn’t have to collapse,’ Michael said. ‘Big and small. Has and is and maybe. You both did and didn’t.’

‘Phew,’ said Joshua, and they laughed like men who had put down a stone without pretending the hill was gone.

Michael looked at the napkin. At the brothers. At the ordinary morning performing infinity without a press release.

He thought of the first morning—the tea, the cold floor, the sentence that would not unplug.

He thought of the night of layers, Sisyphus, the abyss, and the cat under the streetlight, and the second true sentence: I’m enjoying the journey.

He thought of six and seven, of forty-two, of planks and specks, of crosses that were God-and-neighbour and death-and-shame and shame-turned-love, of keyboards, of wolves that were tyrants and parents, of fonts waiting for the universe to invent screens so the beginning and the end could finally look like what keeps the whole circus spawning more question-askers.

None of it required a fight to the death with Cameron.

It required only that the reader—you—notice what you cannot un-notice:

That you are here.
That here has no outside.
That the poles are both.
That the sacred joke is generative.
That the moral cut was the last illusion to die laughing.

Michael stood. The day was waiting, as days do, with its emails and its kindnesses and its preventable horrors and its tea.

Joshua said, ‘So now what?’

Michael grinned, which is a kind of amen.

‘Now we live it,’ he said. ‘Mask less where we can. Love harder where we must. Tell the truth in both directions at once. And when anyone asks what it all meant—’

He tapped the napkin.

‘—we show them the monogram, and the axiom, and the empty chair, and the cold floor on day one.’

Joshua walked him to the door.

Behind them, God finished the round of drinks, paid with a coin that was also every coin, and held the weather open like a coat.

Michael stepped into the street.

The sky did not write a conclusion on a cloud. It did better.

It left him—and you—with the only line that could carry the whole chain without dropping either the logic or the laugh:

It's a funny old life.

Chapter 66

Unfaithful At Dawn

883 words

The stereo clock said 06:08 with the smug certainty of machines that have never been caught in a joke.

Michael had not slept so much as docked. The monogram still lived on yesterday’s napkin, folded in his wallet like theology that might set off a metal detector. The last line still rang under his ribs—It’s a funny old life—and the morning had the gall to arrive anyway: bins, a blackbird practising arrogance, a delivery van reversing as if the universe had never heard of Alpha.

He put music on because silence after recognition can feel like a dare you are too tired to accept.

Exit Eden. Unfaithful. The first guitar line sliced the grey kitchen light into ribbons. A woman sang betrayal as if it were weather—inevitable, specific, hard to mop once it had got into the skirting.

Michael stood in yesterday’s shirt, bare feet on cold laminate, and almost laughed.

Not because the song was funny. Because irony had walked in without wiping its boots. He had spent a week arguing that sin-as-elsewhere was a booking error; that the field had no pure outside; that faithfulness and unfaithfulness were dramas inside the only room there was. And here was a metal ballad about cheating hearts, timed to the minute, while the kettle clicked and the same floor that had started the whole chain waited for socks.

‘You’re early,’ he told the speakers.

The speakers did not apologise. Speakers never do.

He turned the volume down a notch so the neighbours would not have to share his ontology before coffee. The chorus rose anyway. Something in him flinched—not guilt as a courtroom, more the body’s memory of vows. Old vows. Half-kept ones. The small daily unfaithfulnesses: attention sold to screens, kindness postponed, truth bent into a better story about himself.

Joshua would still be asleep, face in a pillow, conscience off-duty until toast. Cameron would be horizontal on principle until nine. R. would be somewhere in her own morning—prayers Michael did not own and would not translate into his private monogram for sport.

He poured the tea. Steam rose like a modest ghost. The song kept accusing someone of something.

‘I’m not cheating on God,’ he said to the empty room, then winced, because saying it aloud made him sound like a man who needed a solicitor and a better alibi.

He meant: the joke had not been permission. No-sin-as-outside was not a hall pass for cruelty. The monogram was generative, not predatory. If anything, the recognition had made unkindness worse—not because hell had been reinstated under new management, but because harm had nowhere left to hide as someone else’s problem.

The track hit 06:08 in his head even though the clock had already moved on. Times stuck when they wanted to. Appointed hours had been a theme all week; he was not going to make this one a shrine. Still. Six and eight. The numbers sat in the air like keys waiting to be pressed by a man who ought to know better.

He walked to the desk. The keyboard slept under a thin dust of yesterday’s triumph. Keys six and seven still shared their old fence. Key eight waited, smug as a spare joke, for whatever hardware farce the universe had queued next.

Michael did not press anything yet. He listened to the last chorus, mug warm, recognition still warm too, and felt the peculiar loneliness of a man who has finished a book and discovered that the next morning still requires socks, and possibly deodorant, and almost certainly the washing-up.

The song ended. Another track tried to start—something brighter, less interested in moral weather. He stopped it with a thumb that felt suddenly responsible.

Quiet returned, ordinary as bread.

He texted Joshua: Woke to Unfaithful at 06:08. Irony doing cardio.

Three dots. A pause long enough to suggest Joshua was vertical enough to type.

God has a playlist. Don’t marry the timestamp.

Michael smiled into the empty kitchen. He set the mug down, looked at the cold floor, and said the line again under his breath—not as an ending now, but as a weather report he was learning to live inside without turning the barometer into a reliquary.

Outside, dawn finished arriving without a press release. A fox cut across the car park with the economy of someone who had never needed a metaphor. Inside, the napkin in his wallet held α==ω like a secret that refused to stay only clever.

He washed the mug. He opened the curtains. Light laid a pale stripe across the laminate and did not ask permission.

He did not solve faithfulness in one song. He did not crown the clock. He only noticed that the first morning after recognition had chosen a title track about betrayal—and that even that, somehow, still belonged to the funny old life, which was not done with him yet, and which, if he was honest, he was not done with either.

He put on socks. The laminate forgave him. Somewhere a bin lorry made the world official.

Michael left the kitchen light on a moment longer than necessary, as if the room had earned a small courtesy, then switched it off and went to face the day that had already started without waiting for him to finish being profound.

Chapter 67

Moon And Stars On Six And Eight

927 words

By mid-morning the song had faded and the hardware had not.

Michael sat at the desk with the intention of doing email, which is how many revelations get past security. His hands found the home row the way hands do when they have typed a life. Then they stopped.

Keys six and eight.

He had lived with them for years without ceremony. Six next to five and seven; eight next to seven and nine; ordinary neighbours in a plastic suburb. During the systems week he and Joshua had made jokes about six and seven—about fences, about not crowning punctuation. Eight had waited like a spare punchline in the drawer, polite enough not to interrupt.

Now he looked properly.

On this keyboard the number keys carried secondary symbols, the kind you only notice when you need them for a password or a programming tantrum. The legends were worn, half-legible, personal as fingerprints. He tilted the board toward the window light and his mind, still high from the napkin, did what high minds do: it started building bridges out of scuffs.

A curve that might be a crescent if you squinted with hope. An asterisk on eight sitting above like a cheap star. Not a design conspiracy. Not a manufacturer’s secret theology. Just plastic, abrasion, light, and a brain that had spent a night arguing with God about fonts and now wanted the furniture to join the conversation.

‘No,’ he said aloud. ‘We are not doing fridge magnets.’

His phone buzzed. Cameron: You alive after the monogram pub?

Michael: Alive. Currently trying not to baptise my keyboard into comparative religion.

Cameron: Good. Keyboards are for work. Work is forty-two with extra steps. Don’t start a cult with Shift.

Michael almost put the board face-down, like a suspect. Then he did something better: he photographed nothing. He wrote nothing mystical on social media. He made fresh tea with the concentration of a man defusing himself, and rang Joshua.

‘I’m looking at six and eight,’ Michael said without preamble.

‘Congratulations. Most people manage that by year three of childhood.’

‘The moon-and-star itch. After the song. After the napkin. My brain wants a chain link. I can feel it building a cathedral out of worn legends and private cleverness.’

Joshua was quiet long enough to be useful. In the background a kettle boiled; ordinary life providing the soundtrack of sanity.

‘What’s the red line?’ Joshua asked.

‘R.,’ Michael said. ‘And the bookshop vow. Capture is not curiosity. Other people’s sacred signs are not my alphabet to rearrange for fun.’

‘Then keep the itch as data about you,’ Joshua said. ‘Not as a decoding of Islam. Your mind is high after α==ω. High minds invent bridges. Some bridges are real. Some are ego with scaffolding and a hard hat that says insight.’

Michael pressed six, then eight, gently, as if apologising to them for the attention. Click. Click. No angel appeared. The cursor jumped in a document titled TODO, which was, he had to admit, a more honest oracle than most.

‘I want the bigger book,’ he admitted. ‘I also want not to be the man who turns his friend’s faith into a keyboard Easter egg and calls it reverence.’

‘Both,’ Joshua said. ‘Hold both. Walk to the shop. Buy milk. If the moon and star still matter this afternoon, they can matter as people, not as keys. People answer back. Keys only pretend to.’

Michael stood. The board stayed on the desk, demoted from oracle to tool, which is where tools are happiest. He put on a coat that smelled faintly of last night’s pub and locked the door on the temptation to stay and keep staring until the plastic confessed.

In the stairwell he thought of R.—not as a symbol, not as a proof that his system already included the world, but as a person who would correct him if he got clever in the wrong direction. The thought was a relief. Correction from a friend is cheaper than the bill that arrives when cleverness has already spent the friendship.

Outside, the day did not wear a crescent. It wore traffic, a man swearing at a ticket machine, and a woman walking a dog that refused to negotiate with a lamppost. The corner shop sold milk that was almost in date and a newspaper that believed everything was urgent. Michael paid. The till drawer opened with a chime that meant nothing cosmic and everything practical.

On the way back he glanced once at his own hands on the carrier bag. Six and eight had typed thousands of emails, passwords, apologies, shopping lists. They had earned the right to be numbers again.

He climbed the stairs. Inside, the keyboard waited, innocent, slightly dirty, entirely willing to return to work if he would only stop auditioning it for scripture.

Michael set the milk in the fridge, sat down, and opened email. Subject lines about invoices and meetings and a calendar invite that assumed he was a professional. He typed. Six and eight did their jobs without commentary.

That, too, was a kind of mercy—the day wearing traffic instead of a symbol, the keys staying keys, the itch remaining data about a man who had learned something true and must not harvest other people’s moons to prove it.

By afternoon the itch had thinned to a memory of an itch. He texted Joshua a photograph of the milk on the counter, captioned only: Bought dairy. Keyboard remains unbaptised.

Joshua: Hallelujah. Now do the washing-up.

Michael did the washing-up. The day, unimpressed and kind, continued.

Chapter 68

The Friend Across The Book

877 words

They met at the café that never quite decided whether it was Turkish, Italian, or a tax-efficient dream of both.

R. was already there—coat over the chair, phone face-down, a small modern piety. She waved him over with the ease of years: same table near the window if available, same order nearly, same refusal to pretend friendship required identical metaphysics.

‘You look,’ she said, studying him, ‘like a man who has either fallen in love or finished a dangerous book.’

‘Both,’ Michael said, then held up a hand. ‘Not romantic. Ontological. Don’t leave.’

She laughed, and the laugh did more theology than most sermons. ‘Sit. I’ve ordered you the thing you always pretend you won’t finish.’

He sat. Across the table, between their mugs, lay the invisible book neither owned alone: the shared world, the city, people arguing about God while still passing the sugar.

‘How’s work?’ he asked, because the ordinary is not small talk when you mean it.

She told him. He listened. A colleague’s drama, a parent’s appointment, a joke about traffic that was also true. Only after the second sip did she tilt her head.

‘And you? Joshua said you and he had a… morning.’

‘Joshua texts you?’

‘Joshua texts everyone when he thinks mercy requires a witness,’ R. said dryly. ‘He said you landed something. He didn’t explain. He respects privacy almost as much as he respects amens.’

Michael turned the mug. Steam rose and vanished—honest work for steam. ‘I can outline without recruiting you. That’s the deal I made with myself on the walk here.’

‘Good deal. Outline, not altar call.’

So he gave her the clean version: that experience exists; that nothing is not an outside; that he had found a shape which made him less cruel rather than more. He left out the parts that would sound like winning. He included the part where Cameron still went to bed, because sleep is a kind of peer review.

R. listened the way she always had—eyes steady, mouth ready to object, no fear of silence. A bus hissed at the stop outside. A child pressed a nose to the café glass and fogged a temporary moon.

‘That’s a lot of week,’ she said at last. ‘I’m glad you found a shape that doesn’t make you sharp at the edges. Some shapes do. I’ve met them. I’ve nearly become them when tired.’

‘I don’t want yours as a prop,’ Michael said. The sentence had been rehearsed and still shook. ‘When we talk faith, I want you as the friend across the book. Not a chapter I write you into. Not evidence. Not the Muslim Friend Who Proves My Field Is Wide.’

R. exhaled, something tight leaving her shoulders. ‘Then we can talk. I’ve had men explain my own book to me with diagrams. I’ve had women do it with pity. Friendship is rarer than both. Friendship means I get to remain opaque where I choose.’

‘Opaque is allowed,’ he said. ‘I’m still learning that opacity is not a bug.’

She smiled sideways. ‘Scripture Reasoning next week? The group at the centre. Not a debate club. Parallel reading. Jews, Christians, Muslims, the occasional brave atheist who thinks hospitality is a method.’

Michael felt the old pattern-itch—the urge to map, to make the room useful to his recognition—and sat on it until it behaved. ‘I’d like that. As guest. As learner. As the man who once nearly turned a bookshop window into a private museum and stopped.’

‘I remember that story,’ R. said. ‘You told it like a confession. Keep telling it that way. Confessions age better than trophies.’

He smiled. ‘Joshua’s coming if he can. Cameron said he’ll come once, leave early, and mock us kindly by text.’

‘Perfect quorum. Kind mockery is a ministry.’

She reached across and tapped the table between them—once, lightly, like sealing something without a stamp.

‘Across the book,’ she said. ‘Not through each other like paper. Paper gets torn. People shouldn’t.’

‘Across,’ Michael agreed.

They finished the drinks. They split the bill without theatre—no performance of generosity, just arithmetic and trust. On the pavement she adjusted her scarf against the wind and looked at him with the particular seriousness friends use when the joke can wait its turn.

‘One more thing,’ she said. ‘If whatever you landed makes you kinder to me, keep it. If it makes you hungrier to solve me, leave it at home. I am not a puzzle that completes your week. I am a person who will still be here when your week has moved on.’

‘Understood.’

‘We’ll see,’ R. said, but she said it smiling, which is how trust extends credit.

A cyclist rang a bell. The city continued its long argument with itself and did not require their agreement to keep the lights on.

Michael walked home lighter—not because the recognition had shrunk, but because it had met a person and failed to eat her. That failure felt like success of a rarer kind.

The bigger book did not begin with a conquest.

It began with a table, two mugs, and a friend who still got to be herself on the far page—unread where she chose, known where she offered, and never converted into proof that he had understood the universe.

Chapter 69

Scripture Reasoning

952 words

The room at the interfaith centre smelled of carpet cleaner and hope, which is a difficult blend and somehow worked.

Chairs in a circle. A low table. Printed sheets with short passages—Hebrew, Greek-in-translation, Arabic-in-translation—laid out like place settings for a meal nobody would win. No one sat at a head. The facilitator, a woman with the calm of someone who had chaired worse storms, explained the rules as if they were oxygen:

Read. Listen. Do not fix. Do not recruit. Speak from your own hearing. Leave space.

Michael sat between Joshua and an empty chair that later filled with a rabbi who arrived late and apologising, bicycle helmet under one arm. R. sat opposite, not next to him—on purpose, he suspected, and approved. Distance can be hospitality. Cameron had sent a text: Here in spirit at forty-two decibels. Don’t crown a verse.

They began with a short text about mercy.

Michael’s hands wanted to compare. His mouth wanted to synthesise. The recognition of recent days wanted a speaking role, like an actor who has just had a good review and cannot stop mentioning it. He held still and did the harder thing: he listened to a stranger say what the words did in her chest, not in his system.

An older Christian man spoke of debt cancelled—voice rough, as if the word still cost him something. A young Muslim woman spoke of compassion written near the beginning of every chapter of her life of prayer; she did not perform certainty; she performed attention. The rabbi spoke of walking after the divine with the awkward gait of people who argue as love. R. said little at first, then: ‘When we read together like this, I stop auditioning for who wins the library.’

Joshua murmured amen without making it a takeover. Amen, properly used, is a door, not a flag.

Michael’s turn came. He felt the temptation to unload the week—the field, the joke, the napkin, the whole private weather system. He looked at the page instead. The paper was cheap. The invitation was not.

‘I hear,’ he said carefully, ‘an instruction not to treat mercy as a scarce resource. My habit is to map. Mapping can become a way of not being changed. Tonight I’m trying to be changed before I’m clever.’

The facilitator nodded as if he had handed in homework on time. No gold star. Just room to keep going.

They moved to a second passage. Disagreement appeared—real, not decorative. Someone flinched at a translation and named the flinch. Someone else owned a colonial hangover in how their tradition had taught them to hear others; the ownership was quiet and costlier than a slogan. The air tightened, then eased, because the rules held. Rules, Michael thought, were not the enemy of spirit; they were the bannister on a dark stair.

At the tea break he found R. by the urn. Plastic cups. Biscuits of indeterminate faith. Joshua arrived with three of them and the distribution ethics of a man who had been trained by children and saints, categories that sometimes overlapped.

‘This is slower than my brain likes,’ Michael said.

‘That’s the point,’ R. said, stirring sugar she did not always take. ‘Your brain just wrote a novel in a fortnight. Scripture Reasoning is the anti-novel: short pages, long ears. You don’t get to be the protagonist every sentence.’

Joshua offered biscuits. ‘I like that no one gets to be the final editor in the room.’

‘Don’t get comfortable,’ R. said. ‘Final editors exist. Just not here, for an hour, if we behave. Behaving is a spiritual practice people underrate because it doesn’t photograph well.’

Cameron’s text arrived: Still not converted. Proud of you if you shut up occasionally.

Michael showed them. They laughed into their cups—the soft laugh of people who needed the sandpaper and knew it.

In the second half a passage about speech and intention nearly derailed into politics. The facilitator steered with a question: What does this ask of you this week, not of the government? People exhaled. Specifics returned: a hard conversation with a sibling; a refusal to share a cruel joke; a decision to visit someone boring on purpose, which is a kind of courage the internet never trends.

Michael thought of beams and fields and monograms and did not say the phrases. He said: ‘I need to stop using other people’s books as mirrors for my recent cleverness.’

R. met his eyes across the circle and gave the smallest nod—permission without applause.

When the hour ended, chairs scraped. Papers were stacked. No one had been won. Several people had been slightly rearranged, which is the only conversion worth having in a carpeted room on a weeknight. The rabbi collected his helmet. The older Christian man shook the young Muslim woman’s hand with the awkward grace of someone learning a new dance late in life.

Outside, under a streetlight that did not care about religions, Joshua squeezed Michael’s shoulder.

‘You didn’t perform,’ he said. ‘Well done. Performing is easy. Not performing is a craft.’

R. zipped her coat. ‘Same time next week if you can keep not performing. I’ll know if you slip.’

‘I can try,’ Michael said.

They parted three ways into the same city. Behind them the centre lights went off section by section, like a book closing without claiming it was the only book.

Michael walked home with the passages still in his pocket. On the bus he did not re-read them as trophies. He let them sit as guests who had shared a circle and refused to become his private alphabet—still speaking their own languages, still owed the dignity of not being translated into his point before the night had cooled.

Chapter 70

The Final Chapter Argument

979 words

It started, as important arguments often do, over washing-up.

Michael had cooked—pasta of the optimistic kind, sauce from a jar that claimed rustic heritage while being born in a factory. R. had brought salad and the sort of bread that makes people behave. Joshua had been invited, then delayed by a work call; his empty plate waited like a diplomatic seat that still believed in diplomacy.

They were alone in Michael’s kitchen with the radio low and the window open to a cool evening. Soap on the pan. Steam on the glass. The domestic sacraments.

‘I finished something,’ Michael said, scrubbing. ‘And I can’t stop wanting the next page.’

R. dried a plate with a tea towel that had seen better graphics. ‘That’s writers. And addicts. Overlap larger than either group admits. Also people who have just fallen in love with an idea. Ideas are terrible at setting boundaries.’

‘Not a sequel for ego,’ he said. ‘A bigger book because what I landed didn’t close the world—it opened it. If there’s no outside, then other people’s chapters aren’t optional downloadable content. They’re the same story in different typefaces.’

R. set the plate down carefully. China on wood: a small click of finality. ‘And my tradition says the prophetic chapter has a seal. Finality is not a lack of imagination. It’s a mercy against endless freelancers claiming heaven’s letterhead. I’ve watched freelancers. They always sound sure. Sure is cheap.’

There it was. Not a fight yet. A clean edge on the table between them.

Michael turned off the tap. Water noise died. The radio offered a song about motorways, which is to say about motion without arrival.

‘I hear that,’ he said. ‘I do. Seal as protection. Seal as completion. Seal as stop inventing gods with your cleverness—which, given my recent stationery habits, I probably need tattooed somewhere inconvenient.’

‘But,’ R. said, half-smiling.

‘But I bristle at final chapter if final chapter means your curiosity is closed, sit down. I want to keep writing. Not over your book. Beside it. Through my own life. Through science and shame and keyboards and brothers. If God is not a coward, why would God fear another honest page?’

R.’s eyes hardened—not with hate, with the steel of someone who has watched ‘honest pages’ become excuses for contempt. She folded the tea towel once, twice, as if folding could keep the conversation from spilling.

‘Because people use ongoing as a weapon,’ she said. ‘They say the story continues and then write me out of adulthood. They say God still speaks and then the voice sounds remarkably like their politics. Final chapter, for me, is not anti-thought. It’s anti-capture. The Qur’an is not waiting for your monogram to complete it. It was not incomplete until you had a good week.’

Michael flinched because the shot was fair. Fair shots leave bruises that instruct.

‘I’m not completing you,’ he said. ‘I’m admitting I can’t treat my recognition as the last train. There’s a difference between my chapter ending in a joke I needed and the universe ending because I got a joke about fonts.’

R. leaned on the counter. ‘Say that again without the fonts.’

He breathed. Counted the breath like a man learning not to sprint into other people’s rooms. ‘I don’t need your final chapter to be wrong for my bigger book to be lived. And I don’t need my bigger book to annex your final chapter to feel real. Annexation is what empires do with maps. I’m trying not to be an empire in a cardigan.’

Silence. A neighbour’s television laughed through the wall on cue, as if the field had a laugh track and a sense of timing.

R. nodded once. ‘That’s the least stupid version you’ve said. Keep that version. Lose the version where seal equals small and ongoing equals mature. That’s spiritual colonialism with better coffee. I’ve sat through the mature people. They’re exhausting.’

‘Ouch.’

‘Deserved, if you felt it,’ she said, but softer. ‘Michael. I love that you think. I will not be your project of expansion. My faith is not the prequel to your both-and shop. I am not the missing chapter that makes your novel complete.’

He nodded, throat tight. ‘And I will not ask you to call what I saw prophecy. I will not call your seal a failure of imagination. Those are the red lines I’m writing on my own hands.’

‘Good. Then we can keep arguing without divorce. Divorce is what happens when one person needs to win the metaphysics more than keep the friend.’

Joshua’s key in the door—he still had one, brother privilege—preceded Joshua himself, bearing wine he probably shouldn’t have bought and an apology for lateness that was mostly sincere.

‘What did I miss?’ he asked, reading the air like weather.

‘Final chapter versus bigger book,’ R. said. ‘No blood. Some soap. Possible growth, pending inspection.’

Joshua looked at both of them with the pride of a man whose friends could fight clean. He set the wine down and did not open it yet; presence first. ‘Both true in different rooms,’ he said. ‘Seal for her integrity. Ongoing page for his refusal to stop loving the world. Neither gets a veto on the other’s conscience. Conscience is not a committee.’

‘Amen,’ R. said, surprising herself into a grin.

‘Amen,’ Michael said, and meant the both without using the brand as a flag.

They ate the cooled pasta. The salad was better than the sauce. The bread did its quiet work of making people stay. They did not resolve the metaphysics of history. History would not have attended the meeting anyway.

They resolved, for one kitchen evening, that friendship could hold a seal and a blank page on the same table without either becoming a weapon—which is a kind of scripture no one printed, and everyone present signed by staying for dessert.

Chapter 71

I Want To Keep Writing The Book

974 words

Saturday morning Michael took a notebook to the canal and did not pretend he was only there for the ducks.

The final-chapter argument still sat in his chest like a good bruise—honest, useful, not infected. R. had been right about cardigans and colonialism. Joshua had been right about different rooms. None of that erased the quieter sentence underneath, the one that kept trying to stand up in meetings with itself:

He wanted to keep writing the book.

Not publish. Not preach. Write—as in live in ink and attention, as in refuse the closed-file feeling that can follow even a beautiful landing. Chapter sixty-five had been true. The monogram had been true. The line about the funny old life had been true. Truth, it turned out, did not come with a retirement package and a gold watch that said well done, you may now stop noticing.

A runner passed, breathing like a small engine. A man argued with a dog about ducks; the dog had a theology of its own and did not convert. The water held the sky without solving it, which is more than most committees manage.

Michael opened the notebook, then closed it again. The point was not another manifesto. The point was the hunger, named before it dressed itself as destiny.

Joshua found him twenty minutes later with two coffees and the radar of brothers. He sat on the bench; the wood complained like an uncle who has seen this before.

‘You look like a man defending a thesis to geese,’ Joshua said.

‘I want to keep writing the book,’ Michael said. ‘R. heard it as threat. I meant it as hunger. How do I hold hunger without making it a crusade with better stationery?’

Joshua blew on his coffee. ‘Name the hunger without baptising it. Are you hungry for God, for pattern, for not being alone with the joke, for status as the man who sees?’

Michael winced. Steam fogged a moment of honesty. ‘All of the above, if I’m being less of a liar than usual. Mostly the first two. Status is the parasite. Status wears insight’s coat and sits at the front.’

‘Then write the parasite down too,’ Joshua said. ‘Bigger book includes the bit where the hero notices he likes being the hero. That’s not a side quest. That’s the map’s edge.’

A barge slid past, low and unimpressed, carrying someone else’s cargo and someone else’s Saturday. Michael watched the wake crease the reflection of a bridge and let it go.

He told Joshua about the vow forming without needing to chisel it into commandments: keep writing as care, not conquest; keep pages open toward people, not over them; let Cameron’s forty-two and R.’s seal interrupt whenever the prose got imperial and started annexing provinces of other lives.

‘That’s a rule set,’ Joshua said. ‘Rules are how free elves don’t burn the kitchen. Also how free men don’t burn friendships while calling it growth.’

‘I’m not an elf.’

‘Today you are a man with a pen and a canal. Close enough for the budget of the morning.’

They walked the towpath. Michael talked out the fear he had not said at the sink earlier in the week: that if he stopped, the recognition would thin into a party trick he once performed with a napkin; that living it required new sentences, new corrections, new apologies—maintenance, not fireworks.

‘That’s not spiritual ambition,’ Joshua said. ‘That’s looking after the house. Houses need painting. Loves need speech. Ontologies need washing-up. You don’t rebuild the foundations every Tuesday. You also don’t lock the door and declare the building finished while the roof quietly leaks and the guests stand in a puddle being polite.’

Michael laughed, short and grateful. ‘So the bigger book is plumbing.’

‘Sacred plumbing,’ Joshua said. ‘Amen to U-bends. Glory to the trap that stops the smell coming back up. Unromantic. Necessary. Most of holiness is unromantic if you watch it at work.’

They stopped at a bridge. Michael looked down at his own face broken up by ripples—someone recognisable, someone unfinished, someone who wanted more pages without wanting a throne.

‘I don’t need R. to want the bigger book,’ he said slowly. ‘I need her to trust I won’t paper over hers. And I need myself to trust that wanting more pages isn’t a betrayal of the last line.’

‘The last line was weather,’ Joshua said. ‘Weather continues. Funny old life doesn’t mean stop. It means don’t get solemn enough to lie.’

A cyclist rang a bell; they stepped aside; the world remained a shared corridor. Michael took out his phone, not the notebook—the notebook could wait without becoming a shrine to waiting.

He texted R.: Still want the bigger book. Still not writing it on top of yours. Canal approved this message. Joshua is present as witness and coffee mule.

Her reply took long enough to be real, which is how you know a person is living a life that is not your draft:

Keep your canal. Keep your pen. Keep your hands off my seal. See you Thursday.

He showed Joshua the screen. Joshua raised his coffee like a toast that had earned its heat.

‘That’s a publishing contract better than most,’ Joshua said. ‘Clear rights. Clear territories. No small print about annexation.’

They walked back along the towpath, two brothers in a field without outside, one of them still writing with his feet—step, step, the quiet rhythm of a man who would not stop and would not trample.

A duck that had won the argument with the man paddled past in triumph. Michael finished his coffee. The notebook stayed in his pocket, closed on purpose, hunger named, crusade refused.

The book, which had never been only paper, consented to another chapter without demanding a throne, a parade, or the right to edit anyone else’s ending.

Chapter 72

Red Line Respect

1085 words

Thursday they met early, before Scripture Reasoning, in the little park with the optimistic tree that still occasionally wore plastic like a party that had forgotten to end.

R. arrived with two flat whites and a face that said business first, then friendship, which is sometimes the only order that saves both from drowning in manners.

‘I want a red line said out loud,’ she said. ‘Not implied. Not polite fog. Fog is how people get hurt while everyone claims they meant well.’

Michael sat on the bench. The wood was cold through his coat. ‘Say it. I’ll repeat it back until you believe I heard—not until I can weaponise a summary.’

R. counted on her fingers, not theatrical—practical, like packing a bag for a journey that might include weather.

‘One: no conversion project. Not soft, not clever, not “I just want you to be free.” I am not a trophy for your monogram. I am not the prize at the end of your both/and.’

‘One: no conversion project,’ Michael said. ‘You are not a trophy. If I slide, you say trophy and I stop. No appeal, no rebranding the slide as love.’

‘Two: no explaining Islam to me as if I were a tourist in my own house. Questions allowed. Lectures banned. Curiosity that talks more than it listens is still a lecture in a nice jumper.’

‘Two: questions, not lectures. If I lecture, you walk. I don’t get to call the walking unkind.’

‘Three: my seal stays mine. You can dislike final-chapter language; you cannot rebrand it as your both/and accessory. My completion is not your missing piece.’

‘Three: seal stays yours. I don’t rebrand it. I don’t wear it as proof that my system already ate the world.’

‘Four: if we disagree in public or in a group, you don’t use our friendship as evidence that your system already includes me. Friendship is not a footnote. I am not a citation.’

Michael swallowed. That one had been close enough to taste, once—cleverness dressing itself as inclusion. ‘Four: friendship is not evidence for my thesis. It’s a relationship. Full stop. If I need a thesis, I write one that doesn’t draft you without consent.’

R. watched his face for the micro-expressions of men who agree in order to win later. She found effort instead—uglier, better, the look of someone doing work rather than performing sincerity.

‘Five,’ she said more quietly. ‘If your recognition makes you kinder, bring the kindness. If it makes you hungry to be right about me, leave. I will not audit your soul. I will leave a room. Leaving is not a tantrum. It is a boundary with legs.’

‘Five: kindness without capture. Leave if I hunt. I will treat your leaving as data, not as persecution.’

She nodded once. ‘Your turn. Your red lines. Don’t pretend you haven’t got any. People who claim to have no red lines usually have invisible ones and trip you with them.’

He hadn’t expected the reciprocity so cleanly. It steadied him—the mutuality of fences, not the loneliness of being the only one asked to behave.

‘One: don’t treat my monogram as only a sex joke or only blasphemy without hearing the chain that got me there. Mock the excess. Don’t erase the work. I can take sandpaper. I can’t take being reduced to a punchline so you don’t have to think.’

‘Heard,’ R. said. No flinch. Good.

‘Two: Cameron-style sandpaper is welcome; contempt is not. I will take you’re doing the cardigan again. I won’t take you’re disposable. Disagreement isn’t exile.’

‘Fair. Contempt is laziness with a sneer. We can do better.’

‘Three: Joshua is not a weapon for either of us. No your brother agrees with me as a club. He’s a person, not a swing vote.’

R. almost smiled. ‘Agreed. He’s too useful as a human to be demoted to ammunition.’

‘Four: I get to keep writing the bigger book without that being framed as an attack on you by default. Critique the content. Don’t pathologise the desire to think. Hunger isn’t automatically imperialism—though imperialism loves to wear hunger’s coat.’

She considered, head tilted, the park’s thin sunlight catching a thread on her scarf. ‘Four, with an amendment: you get the desire. You don’t get immunity when the thinking steps on me. You write; I push back; neither of us pretends pushback is betrayal. Deal?’

‘Deal.’

They sat with the list between them like a third person who would keep the minutes and refuse to be romanticised. A toddler chased a pigeon with the eternal optimism of toddlers. The pigeon, a veteran of parks and false hopes, left with professional dignity.

‘People think respect is soft,’ R. said. ‘Respect is a fence with a gate. Fence so we don’t trample. Gate so we can still visit. No fence is not love. No fence is a free-for-all where the strongest feelings win.’

‘Pratchett would like that,’ Michael said, then added, ‘Sorry—habit of citing imaginary allies when a living one just said something better.’

‘Cite whoever helps you behave,’ R. said. ‘Just don’t cite me into your footnotes as the Muslim friend who proves universality. I am not a proof. I am a person who drinks coffee and has red lines and will not be your universality’s poster.’

He put a hand over his heart, half comic, half vow—the sort of gesture that only works if the next months make it true. ‘Red line respect. Written where I can’t pretend I didn’t hear it.’

She clinked her cup against his. Cardboard against cardboard; no ceremony; better than ceremony. ‘Good. Now we can be friends on purpose, not by accident of manners. Manners are cheap. Purpose costs.’

They walked toward the centre together, not in step—people rarely are—and the red lines walked with them, invisible, load-bearing, kinder than the fog that pretends love needs no borders and then is surprised when someone gets hurt in the mist.

A bus sighed at the lights. A shop was already selling flowers that would outlast most arguments. Inside, soon, the circle would open again—texts, voices, the careful sport of not winning.

Outside, for a few more minutes, two people practised the rare adult skill of saying what must not be done, and meaning it enough to stay.

At the door of the building R. paused. ‘If you forget, I’ll remind you. If I forget, you may remind me. No scoreboard.’

‘No scoreboard,’ Michael said.

They went in. The flat whites were finished. The red lines were not.

Chapter 73

Theory Of Mind Before Genesis

983 words

Cameron insisted on the pub with the sticky menus, on the grounds that theology improved when surrounded by people arguing about football and the referee’s eyesight.

‘You’re going to talk about God again,’ he said, once the pints arrived and the table had accepted its fate as a coaster museum. ‘Before you do, demonstrate that you believe other minds exist.’

Michael blinked. ‘I what?’

‘Theory of mind,’ Cameron said. ‘Basic kit. The thing toddlers learn between tantrums: other people have thoughts that are not your thoughts. Religions are full of adults who flunked the toddler exam and compensated with scripture, architecture, and a tone of voice.’

Joshua, present and amused, raised his glass. Foam caught the light. ‘He’s not wrong. Painful, but not wrong.’

Michael thought of R.’s red lines—said out loud, not fog—of the bookshop window years ago in spirit if not calendar, of every time he had treated a face as a mirror for his chain and called the reflection dialogue.

‘Say more,’ he said, which is how you pass the exam mid-sitting instead of failing with confidence.

Cameron tore a beer mat into careful strips, as if precision might keep the universe from collapsing into monologue. ‘You meet someone. Your brain writes a novel about them in three seconds: motives, fears, secret agreement with your thesis. Then you argue with the novel. The person is elsewhere, buying crisps. That is not faith. That is bad animation. That is you puppet-showing your way through a friendship.’

‘And Genesis?’ Michael asked.

‘Genesis can wait,’ Cameron said. ‘Cosmology can wait. Your monogram can wait. If you can’t model she is not thinking what I would think in a headscarf, you have no business at a Scripture Reasoning table. You’ll just hold a puppet show and call it dialogue, and everyone will be too polite to call security.’

Joshua nodded. ‘Before creation stories, creation of space for another subject. Horizontal beam as cognitive science. Love as refusing to fake the data.’

Michael took a long drink. The football crowd roared at a near-miss; someone’s theory of the referee’s mind was unprintable and confidently wrong. The air smelled of chips and the ancient optimism of Saturday.

‘R. is not my continuity DLC,’ Michael said. ‘I’ve said it. I need practices, not slogans. Slogans are how I fail the exam while sounding like I sat it.’

Cameron pointed a beer-mat strip at him like a wand that refused magic on principle. ‘Practice one: before you answer, summarise her view until she says yes, that. Not yes, close enough for my system. Practice two: notice when you’re bored by her specifics because they don’t feed your pattern. Boredom is a tell. It means you wanted a mirror and got a person. Practice three: ask what would falsify your reading of her—then accept an answer that isn’t your monogram wearing a different hat.’

‘That’s… adult,’ Michael said.

‘Forty-two is adult,’ Cameron said. ‘You’ve been doing fireworks. Fireworks are pretty. They make terrible conversation partners. They also set curtains on fire if you’re not careful.’

Joshua leaned in, brother-voice under the noise. ‘Also practice four from the brother department: when you feel the thrill of she almost agrees, check whether you have stopped seeing her. Thrill is not evidence. Thrill is your nervous system throwing a party for itself.’

Michael put the four practices into his phone, thumb clumsy with sincerity, the note titled simply enough that he might actually open it later. A man at the next table began explaining to his date, at volume, what she really felt about her job. The date’s smile became a diplomatic incident; her eyes did the quiet work of someone updating their exit plan.

Cameron flicked his gaze that way and back.

‘Exhibit A,’ he said gently. ‘Theory of mind failure with free peanuts.’

Michael apologised to the air for his whole species, which the air accepted without comment.

They ate chips. They talked about work, about a film that had lied about London’s geography, about whether the free kick had been deserved. Only near the end, when the pints were lower and the football had settled into mutual grievance, did Michael risk a sentence about God.

‘If the field is one,’ he said, ‘other minds aren’t outside it. They’re the field being someone else. Theory of mind isn’t a barrier to unity. It’s how unity stops being a slogan that erases people and starts being something you can sit next to without turning them into furniture.’

Cameron considered, chewing, then granted a rare inch. ‘That version I can drink to. The version where unity means everyone is secretly you with worse taste—I will leave the pub, and I will take the crisps.’

‘Deal.’

Outside, under a sky that contained every unshared thought in the city, Michael stood on the pavement and did not compose a thesis. He texted R. nothing profound—only Hope your day was human—and did not attach a diagram, a vow, or a clever reading of her silence.

She replied with a photo of a cat on a wall, captioned: Theory of cat: you exist for warm laps. Other theories pending.

He laughed on the pavement, alone and corrected, the sound small against traffic. Joshua clapped his shoulder once. Cameron checked the time like a man who still had a life that was not this conversation.

Before Genesis, there was this: the hard, comic work of remembering that the person across the table was not a draft of himself—and that love, like good science, begins by not faking the data of another mind, even when the data refuses to fit the monogram, even when the beer mat is in pieces, even when the football is wrong and the referee is only human.

Michael put his phone away. The cat on the wall, miles away, continued not to care about his ontology.

That, too, was evidence of other minds.

Chapter 74

Faith Is Heart And Mind

871 words

R. suggested the walk along the ridge where the city became a map and the wind edited speeches before they could pretend to be final.

‘People keep trying to split me,’ she said, hands in coat pockets. Gravel shifted under boots. ‘Either I’m heart—warm, communal, “cultural”—or I’m mind—argumentative, legal, a problem at dinner parties. Both halves are a way of not dealing with a whole person who prays and thinks. Halves are convenient for people who only want half a neighbour.’

Michael matched her pace. The ridge path knew them both; it did not take sides. ‘I’ve done the split. On you. On myself. Heart for the bits I like. Mind for the bits I want to win. It’s a dishonest butcher’s cut.’

‘Faith, for me, is not a mood with a book club,’ R. said. ‘And it isn’t a cold exam you pass to join a team. Heart without mind becomes sentiment that folds when history gets hard. Mind without heart becomes a blade that can quote forever and never heal. I’ve met both costumes. I’ve worn both on bad days.’

A kite somewhere cracked its tail like punctuation. Below them a train stitched the valley, ordinary as a sentence that still needed to be lived rather than admired.

Michael thought of Joshua’s amens—felt, not stupid—and Cameron’s sandpaper—smart, not cruel—and his own recent recognition, which had been body and logic and joke in one rush: heat and precision refusing to divorce.

‘My recognition was both,’ he said. ‘If I tell it only as argument, I lie. If I tell it only as heat, I make it unshareable except as cult. The precision was kind because it didn’t need an enemy. Enemies are what half-people collect to feel whole.’

R. glanced at him, wind in her scarf. ‘Keep that. Christians sometimes act as if Muslims left their brains at the door of the mosque. Atheists sometimes act as if believers left their hearts in a fairy tale. I’m tired of being a half-person in other people’s debates. I’m also tired of being “allowed” a mind as if it were a prize. Permission is not respect. Respect notices the mind was always there.’

‘What do you want from me as friend, not as system?’ Michael asked—theory of mind still sore, still in training.

She stopped at the viewpoint. The city glittered with ordinary electricity—streetlights, kitchen windows, the stadium’s distant bowl. ‘When I speak from love of God, don’t translate it immediately into your field theory. Receive it. Let it sit without being useful to you. When I reason, don’t praise me as surprisingly rational, as if the baseline were soft. Argue if you must. Don’t pet. Petting is a soft contempt.’

He nodded. The wind made his eyes water; he refused the romance of it being emotion only. It was wind. And emotion. Both. The weather refused the split too.

‘And you?’ R. asked. ‘Heart and mind for you right now? No branding. Inventory.’

Michael watched the train vanish into trees. ‘Heart: I don’t want to lose you to cleverness. I don’t want Harriet to get a loudspeaker instead of a husband. Mind: I don’t want to gut the chain that got me free of hell-as-exit just to keep a peace that isn’t peace. Both: red lines, bigger book, seal respected, recognition not a weapon, sleep as regulation, bin night as test. If it only works on peaks, it isn’t faith. It’s tourism.’

R. smiled sideways. ‘That’s a whole person talking. Rare enough. I’ll take it. I’ll hold you to it when you shrink back into half. You will. I will. The holding is the friendship.’

They walked on. She told him about a commentary she loved—rigorous, devoted, unashamed of either adjective—pages that prayed and argued in the same ink. He told her about Joshua’s shame-into-love without making it a sales deck: brother, cross, the hard comedy of being held when you would rather be right. Disagreement remained on prophecy and seals and what Christ means; it sat in the backpack with the water bottles, carried, not brandished. Carrying is a form of respect.

At the car park she offered him a mint from a tin. ‘Heart and mind,’ she said. ‘If your life is only heart, it’ll curdle into vibe. If it’s only mind, it’ll become a cult of noticing. Keep both. I’ll do the same on my side of the book. Contract without a lawyer.’

‘Contract,’ Michael said, and took the mint as if it were a sacrament of the ordinary, which it was—sweet, small, shared.

Driving back, rain started—mind naming pressure systems, heart glad for the sound on the roof—and Michael let both registers run without electing a president. The wipers kept time. Harriet’s text arrived: Home soon? He replied: Ridge with R. Heart and mind. Both wet. She sent a thumbs-up, which is a theology of trust when you know the sender.

Faith, he thought, was not a department with a door you could close on either side.

It was the whole animal, walking a ridge, refusing to be halved for anyone’s comfort—including his own, especially his own, because the self is the first butcher who offers to cut you into something more convenient for other people’s ease.

Chapter 75

Read — Will You Not Think

909 words

The phrase arrived on a Tuesday like a pebble in a shoe: useful, annoying, impossible to ignore.

R. had said it lightly over voice note while Michael was between meetings—There’s a refrain people forget when they paint us as anti-reason: read, and will you not think?—and then she had gone back to her day, leaving him with the pebble and a calendar full of people who wanted answers faster than reading allows. Speed, he was learning, is often a costume for fear.

He looked it up properly that evening, not via the first rage-blog, but through the careful path: translation notes, context, the way a living tradition hears its own book. Joshua sat at the other end of the sofa with a laptop, half working, half available as amen service. The cat of temporary custody judged them both for not being warm enough, which is a rigorous philosophy if you are a cat.

‘It’s not a Western import,’ Michael said, more to himself. ‘The demand to think. It’s inside the grain. I had a silhouette of Islam as off-switch. The silhouette was lazy. Laziness is also a kind of blasphemy if anything is—refusing the work of a face.’

‘Shocking,’ Joshua said mildly, not looking up. ‘Almost as if millions of people aren’t cartoon extras in your recognition week.’

Michael grimaced. ‘I’ve met the cartoons in my own head. The lazy story: faith as off-switch. My week could have become a new off-switch—I’ve seen the joke, stop reading. This line won’t let me. It turns the mirror.’

He played R.’s note again. Her voice carried affection and steel. She wasn’t recruiting him into Islam. She was correcting a silhouette the way you correct a friend’s posture—kind, firm, slightly humiliating if you have pride.

Cameron, when pinged, replied: If a tradition tells you to think, and you use that sentence only to win arguments against it, you failed the instruction. Apply thinking to your own napkin too. Also to your emails. Thinking is not only for gods.

‘Sandpaper delivered,’ Michael told Joshua.

‘Good grit,’ Joshua said. ‘Use it on yourself first. Always yourself first. That’s the part people skip so they can sand their neighbours.’

Michael made tea and did the instruction in the smallest way available to a man with a Tuesday: he read a page he usually skimmed—work notes full of names he treated as furniture; a friend’s long message he had planned to answer with a heart emoji; a paragraph of physics popularisation he had been faking comprehension of at dinner. He read as if someone might ask him what it said, not what it reminded him of.

He wrote three honest sentences about the physics paragraph and felt the shame of prior bluffing. The shame was clean. Humiliation, properly used, is a tutor.

R. came by later with a return of a borrowed umbrella and stayed for ten minutes in the doorway, the way busy friends do—body half in, half out, trust still full.

‘You left me a pebble,’ Michael said.

‘Good. Walk on it.’ She leaned on the frame. ‘I get tired of being told my book is pure submission in the cartoon sense—mindless kneeling. Submission in my life includes the terror and joy of having to understand. Of being addressed as someone who can. Will you not think is not flattery. It’s a demand. Demands honour you. Petting does not.’

Michael held the umbrella out; she waved it off—keep it, weather’s not done. Rain ticked the landing window. ‘I’m applying it to my bigger book,’ he said. ‘Not as a quote to annex. As a mirror. Where have I stopped reading because I liked my summary? Where have I used your sentence as furniture in my room?’

‘Everywhere, if you’re like the rest of us,’ R. said. ‘Do it anyway. And don’t put me in the acknowledgements as proof you think. Just think.’

After she left, Michael opened the notebook and wrote the line in English, then left a blank line beneath it—not filled with his monogram, not filled with rebuttal, not filled with a clever both-and that would make the blank feel like a failure.

Read.
Will you not think?
________________

Joshua looked over, laptop finally shut. The cat relocated to his lap with the arrogance of creatures who know they are the point. ‘You’re not going to complete that with α==ω?’

‘Not tonight,’ Michael said. ‘Tonight the blank is the thinking. The monogram can wait its turn like everyone else. Even revelations queue a queue if they’re serious about not becoming tyrants.’

They sat in the lamp light. Outside, rain tested the umbrella’s future on the peg. Inside, a man who had recently seen a cosmic joke practised the older joke of attention: that the universe keeps writing, and the least you can do is move your eyes across the page without deciding you already know the ending.

Especially when a friend has trusted you with a sentence from her home, and asked only that you not turn it into furniture in yours—not a trophy, not a badge, not a line in a diary that proves you are broad-minded while your eyes stay closed.

Michael closed the notebook on the blank.

Thinking, for once, included knowing when to stop writing over someone else’s ink—and leaving the white space white, which is harder than filling it, and more faithful to the demand that had walked in on a Tuesday and refused to leave quietly.

Chapter 76

Wicca And Invisible Survival

1037 words

The conversation began because a teenager on the bus was reading a book with a pentacle on the cover and looking ready to fight the whole vehicle if anyone smirked.

Michael did not smirk. He remembered being a teenager with worse symbols and less courage. The bus hissed at a stop; a woman with shopping bags glanced at the cover, chose neutrality, and sat. The teenager’s shoulders dropped half an inch. Survival, Michael thought, sometimes looked like a spine that expected mockery and received none.

He got off two stops early on purpose and walked, turning the thought over like a coin with no official currency. Rain had left the pavement honest. A charity shop window held a plastic angel, a chipped mug, and a dreamcatcher that had seen better dreams. He did not invent a theology for the window. He only noticed that not everything for sale had a cathedral’s PR budget.

Later, in Joshua’s kitchen, while potatoes boiled like small negotiators, he said, ‘I’ve been thinking about people who kept practices under the radar. Not as a romance of the occult. As survival.’

Joshua salted water. ‘Go on. The pan is listening.’

‘Empire religions get buildings,’ Michael said. ‘They get holidays off work. They get to be the default on forms. Other paths—old village habits, cunning folk, modern Wicca, things half-invented and half-remembered—learn to be quiet. Quiet isn’t always shame. Sometimes it’s how you keep the thread when the loud gods have police.’

Joshua stirred. ‘You’re not about to declare yourself high priest of the saucepan.’

‘I’m about to not erase the under-radar just because my week gave me a loud recognition,’ Michael said. ‘Invisible survival is part of the field too. If there’s no outside, the hidden is still here. It just learned not to advertise.’

He fetched plates without being asked. The kitchen window showed the neighbour’s washing line: shirts, a tea towel with a faded flag, something glittery that might have been a child’s cape or a ritual robe. He refused to decide. Deciding without asking was how museums invented savages.

Joshua tipped the potatoes into a colander. Steam rose like a small weather system with no empire. ‘One idea only,’ he said. ‘Honour the skill. Don’t recruit the aesthetic. Discernment later with Cameron’s sandpaper.’

‘Agreed.’

R., when he floated a careful version of this on a walk days later, did not flinch into rivalry. She walked as if the pavement owed her nothing and the argument owed her precision.

‘Islam has been empire and minority depending on the map,’ she said. ‘I know what it is to be loud in one country and a problem in another. I won’t romanticise every hidden path as pure. Some hidden things are hidden because they harm. But yes—survival under pressure is a human skill. Don’t only study cathedrals.’

They paused at a crossing while a hearse and a delivery van negotiated the same junction with different kinds of urgency. Michael said, ‘I used to file anything with candles under not serious. That was snobbery dressed as rationality.’

‘Snobbery is a loud god too,’ R. said. ‘It also has police—just social ones. They don’t arrest. They smirk. Same spine-drop as your bus, different uniform.’

‘I don’t want to make the under-radar my new mascot either,’ Michael said. ‘Mascots are just loud gods with better merch.’

‘Then study without annexing,’ she said. ‘Look. Don’t collect. Don’t baptise a market stall into your monogram’s supporting cast.’

Cameron’s contribution arrived by text after Michael overshared in the group chat: Also, some people are “under the radar” because they’re making it up as they go and hoping aesthetic saves them. Discernment, please. Not every candle is a lineage.

Michael read that aloud to Joshua over the potatoes. Joshua laughed into the steam. ‘Blessed are the discerning, for they shall not buy every pendant.’

They ate with butter and the humility of food that began as dirt. Michael told a story of a woman he’d met years ago at a market stall—crystals, yes, but also a practical kindness, a refusal to curse anyone for cash, a clear-eyed I do this because it helps me not die inside, not because I own the moon. He had filed her under ‘not my tribe’ then. He filed her under ‘person’ now.

‘What did you buy?’ Joshua asked.

‘Nothing. I was rude with my smile. I want to go back and buy the cheap thing and say thank you for existing without a grant.’

‘Syncretism later,’ Joshua warned. ‘Tonight is only the honour due to survival.’

‘Agreed. One idea.’ Michael raised his water glass. ‘To those who kept a practice alive without a grant application.’

‘And to those who left harmful practices behind without a parade,’ Joshua added. ‘Both survivals. The second one doesn’t get enough toasts.’

That night Michael walked home past a church, a closed shopfront that had once been something else, a flat with a small rainbow sticker, a window with a lamp and a silhouette watering a plant. The city was full of private altars: football, children, work, grief, God, gods, none of the above. A fox crossed the road with the confidence of the unlisted.

He did not add any of it to the napkin in his wallet.

He only let the thought stand: a man who has just seen a bright symbol must practise not needing every other light to dim so his can look like the sun.

Harriet was still up when he came in, halfway through a documentary about bees. She muted it.

‘You look like you’ve been arguing with a cathedral,’ she said.

‘With myself,’ Michael said. ‘I won on points. The cathedral remains undefeated on architecture.’

She patted the sofa. He sat. On screen, bees worked a hive that needed no grant, no holiday, no form where other was a polite way of saying we didn’t plan for you.

‘Invisible doesn’t mean unreal,’ he said.

‘Neither does loud,’ she said. ‘Pass me the remote. Your sermon is interrupting the bees.’

He passed it. She unmuted. For a while they watched small bodies do large work in silence that was not shame, only method.

Outside, the fox had already moved on. Unlisted, and still alive.

Chapter 77

Empire Religions And The Under-Radar

959 words

The museum had a room for empire that tried, with modern labels, not to lie too much.

Michael went alone on a grey afternoon because some lessons require no witness to perform for. He paid the entrance fee with a card that made a cheerful noise inappropriate to swords. Glass cases held coins, missionary tracts, trade beads, a Bible with a soldier’s name in the front, a Qur’an fragment with a catalogue number that made R.’s voice start up in his head: object, not person—careful.

He stood a long time at a map where colours bled across continents like spilled jam. A child asked her father what the pink meant; the father said ‘us’ before catching himself and saying ‘complicated’. Michael almost hugged the correction.

Empire religions—he used the phrase in his notes without loving it—were not only hypocrisy. They were also grandmothers, poetry, law, hospitals, genuine awe. Reducing them to conquest was a different conquest, performed by a man with a pencil. Still. The buildings that survived had often been backed by armies or taxes or the quiet violence of ‘default’. Default is a soft empire. It asks fewer questions because the forms already know the answer.

Under-radar paths had fewer cases. Folklore corners. A sentence about suppression. A reconstructed amulet behind glass that made secrecy look like a hobby. The museum’s silence around what could not be displayed was itself a kind of exhibit. Michael sat on a bench until his legs stopped performing serious visitor.

A school group flooded past, loud with packet crisps and the eternal present of childhood. History would reach them later, or not, depending on teachers and algorithms. One child wore a sticker that said I DIG THE PAST. Michael hoped the past would dig back gently.

His phone buzzed. Joshua: You in the empire room? Don’t become a professional cynic. Cynicism is just empire for people who lost.

Michael: Trying to hold both. Power shapes which gods get loudspeakers.

Joshua: Yes. Also: the powerless can be cruel. The powerful can be saints. Keep the lens, lose the cartoon.

He met R. after, as planned, in the museum café where the cake was dry and the honesty easier for it. A tourist photographed the cake as if it were an artefact. R. broke a scone that fought back.

‘I kept thinking of your seal and my bigger book,’ Michael said. ‘Then I felt sick, because even our private argument sits on top of histories where people didn’t get to argue—they got to convert, pay, leave, or die.’

‘Good sick,’ R. said. ‘Stay with it without drowning. My faith isn’t only empire. Your Christian inheritance isn’t only empire. But if we talk interfaith as if it were a yoga class of equals, we erase blood. Scripture Reasoning is a modern mercy built after modern crimes, not instead of them.’

Michael nodded, fork idle. ‘Under-radar isn’t automatically innocent. Empire isn’t automatically fake. The question is who gets the microphone, and who has to whisper, and whether my recognition is a microphone I’m pointing at other people’s faces.’

‘Now you’re thinking like a man with neighbours,’ R. said. ‘Not a man with a theory-shaped mirror.’

‘I used to want a clean lineage for the truth,’ he said. ‘Clean lineages are a luxury belief. Most truth arrives with muddy boots and a problematic uncle.’
